which implies that the formula for four-point conformal blocks conformal blocks presented above [see (
3.11
)] can be used for the
t
-channel with the substitutions
\eta_{2}\leftrightarrow\eta_{4}
and
j\leftrightarrow l
.

It is apparent from the above discussion of the symmetry properties of the OPE coefficients in the three-point basis that the four-point conformal bootstrap equations must also be simpler in the same basis. Starting from (
3.10
), we thus have

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(\eta_{1}){\mathcal{O}}_{j}(\eta_{2}){\mathcal{O}}_{l}(\eta_{4}){\mathcal{O}}_{k}(\eta_{3})}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{({\mathcal{T}}_{12}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{i}})({\mathcal{T}}_{21}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{j}})({\mathcal{T}}_{43}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{l}})({\mathcal{T}}_{34}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{k}})\cdot\sum_{m,n}\sum_{a=1}^{N_{ijm}}\sum_{b=1}^{N_{klm}}{}_{[a|}c_{ijm}{}_{[b|}c_{lkn}G^{nm}\mathscr{G}_{[a|b]}^{ij|m|lk}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{4},\eta_{3})}{(\eta_{1}\cdot\eta_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{i}+\tau_{j})}(\eta_{3}\cdot\eta_{4})^{\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{k}+\tau_{l})}(\eta_{1}\cdot\eta_{3})^{\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{k}-\chi_{l})}(\eta_{2}\cdot\eta_{4})^{\frac{1}{2}(-\chi_{i}+\chi_{j})}(\eta_{1}\cdot\eta_{4})^{\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{i}-\chi_{j}-\chi_{k}+\chi_{l})}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{(-1)^{4\xi_{j}\xi_{l}}({\mathcal{T}}_{14}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{i}})({\mathcal{T}}_{41}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{l}})({\mathcal{T}}_{23}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{j}})({\mathcal{T}}_{32}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{k}})\cdot\sum_{m,n}\sum_{a=1}^{N_{ilm}}\sum_{b=1}^{N_{kjm}}{}_{[a|}c_{ilm}{}_{[b|}c_{jkn}G^{nm}\mathscr{G}_{[a|b]}^{il|m|jk}(\eta_{1},\eta_{4},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})}{(\eta_{1}\cdot\eta_{4})^{\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{i}+\tau_{l})}(\eta_{2}\cdot\eta_{3})^{\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{k}+\tau_{j})}(\eta_{1}\cdot\eta_{3})^{\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{k}-\chi_{j})}(\eta_{2}\cdot\eta_{4})^{\frac{1}{2}(-\chi_{i}+\chi_{l})}(\eta_{1}\cdot\eta_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{i}-\chi_{l}-\chi_{k}+\chi_{j})}},

which transforms into

\begin{split}&\sum_{m,n}\sum_{a,a^{\prime}=1}^{N_{ijm}}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}=1}^{N_{klm}}{}_{[a|}c_{ijm}(R_{ijm})_{aa^{\prime}}{}_{[b|}c_{lkn}(R_{lkm})_{bb^{\prime}}G^{nm}\\
&\qquad\times v^{\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{j}+\chi_{k})}\left(\mathscr{G}_{(a^{\prime}|b^{\prime})}^{ij|m|lk}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{4},\eta_{3})\right)_{\{aA\}\{bB\}\{dD\}\{cC\}}\\
&\qquad=(-1)^{4\xi_{j}\xi_{l}}\sum_{m,n}\sum_{a,a^{\prime}=1}^{N_{ilm}}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}=1}^{N_{kjm}}{}_{[a|}c_{ilm}(R_{ilm})_{aa^{\prime}}{}_{[b|}c_{jkn}(R_{jkm})_{bb^{\prime}}G^{nm}u^{\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{k}+\chi_{l})}\\
&\qquad\phantom{=}\times\left(({\mathcal{A}}_{12})^{n_{v}^{i}}({\mathcal{A}}_{34})^{n_{v}^{l}}({\mathcal{A}}_{12})^{n_{v}^{j}}({\mathcal{A}}_{34})^{n_{v}^{k}}\mathscr{G}_{(a^{\prime}|b^{\prime})}^{il|m|jk}(\eta_{1},\eta_{4},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\right)_{\{aA\}\{dD\}\{bB\}\{cC\}},\end{split}

(5.16)

after contracting with half-projectors to convert from embedding space spinor indices to embedding space vector indices and rewriting the blocks directly in terms of (
3.14
). To obtain purely scalar conformal bootstrap equations, we can contract (
5.16
) with the invariant tensors
{\mathcal{A}}_{12}
,
\epsilon_{12}
and
\Gamma_{12}
, as well as the remaining embedding space coordinates
\bar{\eta}_{3}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}_{12}
and
\bar{\eta}_{4}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}_{12}
, which yields the correct number of independent four-point conformal bootstrap equations in terms of objects that are functions of the conformal cross-ratios
u
and
v
only.

The general strategy outlined above thus generates conformal bootstrap equations in terms of the cross-ratios
u
and
v
, as in position space, but directly in the embedding space. Thus, in the context of the embedding space formalism, there is no need to project back to position space.

6
Algorithm

In this section we expound our full general algorithm for computing the rotation matrices and generating the conformal bootstrap equations. We illustrate each step of the algorithm in the context of the simplest nontrivial example, namely
\left\langle{SSSV}\right\rangle
(assuming
d
large to simplify tensor product decompositions). The algorithm is presented as a list of steps for the four-point function
\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{i}{\mathcal{O}}_{j}{\mathcal{O}}_{l}{\mathcal{O}}_{k}}\right\rangle
. Since the irreducible representations of the external quasi-primary operators are fixed, we assume that their projection operators are known. We give the input data of a more complicated example in Appendix
D
.

To simplify the notation, the final results for
\left\langle{SSSV}\right\rangle
are written in terms of the usual scalar conformal block
{\mathcal{G}}_{(\Delta,\ell)}^{(d,\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})}\equiv{\mathcal{G}}_{(\Delta,\ell)}^{(d,\Lambda,0,0,\Lambda^{\prime},0,0)}
as well as the normalization factor
\omega_{(\Delta,\ell)}^{(d,\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})}\equiv\omega_{(\Delta,\ell)}^{(d,\Lambda,0,\Lambda^{\prime},0)}
with conformal dimension
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{m+\ell}
. For convenience, we define the
s
- and
t
-channel quantities
a_{s}
,
b_{s}
and
a_{t}
,
b_{t}
, respectively, to denote the differences of the conformal dimensions of the external operators. These are
a_{s}=(\Delta_{j}-\Delta_{i})/2
,
b_{s}=(\Delta_{k}-\Delta_{l})/2
and
a_{t}=(\Delta_{l}-\Delta_{i})/2
,
b_{t}=(\Delta_{k}-\Delta_{j})/2
for the two channels.

6.1
Rotation Matrices

1.

Determine the possible infinite towers of exchanged quasi-primary operators appearing in
\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{i}{\mathcal{O}}_{j}{\mathcal{O}}_{l}{\mathcal{O}}_{k}}\right\rangle
by identifying the overlapping set of exchanged operators
{\mathcal{O}}_{m}
for the two different three-point correlation functions
\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{i}{\mathcal{O}}_{j}{\mathcal{O}}_{m}}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{l}{\mathcal{O}}_{k}{\mathcal{O}}_{m}}\right\rangle
.

For
\left\langle{SSSV}\right\rangle
, the two three-point correlation functions of interest are
\left\langle{SS{\mathcal{O}}_{m}}\right\rangle
with
{\mathcal{O}}_{m}\in\{{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}\}
and
\left\langle{SV{\mathcal{O}}_{m}}\right\rangle
with
{\mathcal{O}}_{m}\in\{{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}},{\mathcal{O}}^{{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2}+\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}\}
. Since the only non-vanishing three-point functions with two scalars are those that contain a spin-
\ell
quasi-primary operator and since such an exchanged operator leads to non-vanishing three-point functions with one scalar and one vector, it follows that there is only one infinite tower of exchanged quasi-primary operators with
{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}
for which
n_{v}^{m}=0
and
\xi_{m}=0
.

2.

For each infinite tower of exchanged quasi-primary operators, find the corresponding projection operators using group theory and the complete set of allowed tensor structures in the OPE basis with the help of Table
1
and the Fock conditions.

The projection operator for
\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}
is well-known and is given in (
C.1
).

Since
{\boldsymbol{0}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{0}}\otimes\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}=\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}
, there is only one tensor structure for
\left\langle{SS{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}}\right\rangle
and it is given by

({}_{(1|}t_{ij,m+\ell}^{12})_{\{E\}}^{\phantom{\{E\}}\{F\}}=({\mathcal{A}}_{12E}^{\phantom{12E}F})^{\ell}\to{}_{(1|}t_{ijm}^{12}=1\quad\text{with}\quad i_{1}=0,n_{1}=\ell,

(6.1)

where we have extracted the special part (which is trivial in this case).

Meanwhile, for the other three-point correlation function, namely
\left\langle{SV{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}}\right\rangle
, one has
{\boldsymbol{0}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}\otimes\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}=[{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2}+(\ell-1){\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}]\oplus(\ell+1){\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}\oplus(\ell-1){\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}
, which leads to two tensor structures given by

\begin{gathered}({}_{(1|}t_{ij,m+\ell}^{12})_{B\{E\}}^{\phantom{B\{E\}}\{F\}}={\mathcal{A}}_{12B}^{\phantom{12B}F}({\mathcal{A}}_{12E}^{\phantom{12E}F})^{\ell}\to{}_{(1|}t_{ijm}^{12}={\mathcal{A}}_{12B}^{\phantom{12B}F}\quad\text{with}\quad i_{1}=0,n_{1}=\ell+1,\\
({}_{(2|}t_{ij,m+\ell}^{12})_{B\{E\}}^{\phantom{B\{E\}}\{F\}}={\mathcal{A}}_{12BE}({\mathcal{A}}_{12E}^{\phantom{12E}F})^{\ell-1}\to{}_{(2|}t_{ij,m+1}^{12}={\mathcal{A}}_{12BE}\quad\text{with}\quad i_{2}=1,n_{2}=\ell-1.\end{gathered}

(6.2)

We can straightforwardly identify the special parts and related input data from the form of the respective tensor structures.

3.

Invoking (
4.3
) for the three-point tensorial block, use (
3.8
) to write down the explicit three-point conformal blocks for each tensor structure.

For
\left\langle{SS{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}}\right\rangle
, the sole three-point conformal block associated with the tensor structure (
6.1
) is

\begin{split}(\mathscr{G}_{(1|}^{ij|m+\ell})_{\{E\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})&=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{31}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}})_{\{E\}}^{\phantom{\{E\}}\{E^{\prime}\}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)\{E^{\prime}\}}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell},0)}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})}{R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell},0)}}\\
&=\frac{1}{R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell},0)}}[{\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{31}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})^{\ell}]_{\{E\}},\end{split}

(6.3)

where we have applied (
3.8
) with the appropriate input data and then used (
4.3
).

The same procedure can be followed for the two three-point conformal blocks of
\left\langle{SV{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}}\right\rangle
as dictated by (
6.2
), leading to

\begin{split}&(\mathscr{G}_{(1|}^{ij|m+\ell})_{\{aA\}\{bB\}\{eE\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\\
&\qquad=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{21}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{j}})_{B}^{\phantom{B}B^{\prime}}({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{31}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}})_{\{E\}}^{\phantom{\{E\}}\{E^{\prime}\}}{\mathcal{A}}_{12B^{\prime}}^{\phantom{12B^{\prime}}F}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)\{E^{\prime}\}\{F\}}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell},1)}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})}{R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell},1)}},\end{split}

(6.4)

and

\begin{split}&(\mathscr{G}_{(2|}^{ij|m+\ell})_{\{aA\}\{bB\}\{eE\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\\
&\qquad=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{21}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{j}})_{B}^{\phantom{B}B^{\prime}}({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{31}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}})_{\{E\}}^{\phantom{\{E\}}\{E^{\prime}\}}{\mathcal{A}}_{13B^{\prime}}^{\phantom{13B^{\prime}}E^{\prime\prime}}\\
&\qquad\phantom{=}\times\left[\frac{{\mathcal{G}}_{(\chi_{m+\ell}+1,\ell-1)\{E^{\prime}\}\{E^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime}\}}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell}-1/2,2)}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})}{R_{(\chi_{m+\ell}+1,\ell-1)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell}-1/2,2)}}+g_{E^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell-1)\{E^{\prime}\}}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell},0)}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})}{R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell-1)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell},0)}}\right].\end{split}

(6.5)

For easier readability, we did not expand the blocks using (
4.3
) at this stage, as we will express them in the three-point basis in the next step by following the procedure described in Appendix
B
.

4.

Re-express the previous three-point conformal blocks, which are in the OPE basis, in the three-point basis (
5.9
) to extract the rotation matrix elements.

The OPE tensor structure (
6.1
) implies that the corresponding three-point conformal block in the three-point basis is

(\mathscr{G}_{[1|}^{ij|m+\ell})_{\{E\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{31}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}})_{\{E\}}^{\phantom{\{E\}}\{E^{\prime}\}}[({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})_{E^{\prime}}]^{\ell}.

Therefore, from the three-point conformal block (
6.3
), it is straightforward to conclude that the rotation matrix for
\left\langle{SSO^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}}\right\rangle
is simply

(R_{ij,m+\ell}^{-1})_{11}=\frac{1}{R_{(\Delta,\ell)}^{(d,a_{s},0)}},

(6.6)

by direct comparison with the result in the three-point basis.

For
\left\langle{SV{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}}\right\rangle
, the OPE tensor structures (
6.2
) lead to the associated three-point conformal blocks in the three-point basis given by

\displaystyle(\mathscr{G}_{[1|}^{ij|m+\ell})_{B\{E\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{21}^{{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}})_{B}^{\phantom{B}B^{\prime}}({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{31}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}})_{\{E\}}^{\phantom{\{E\}}\{E^{\prime}\}}({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})_{B^{\prime}}[({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})_{E^{\prime}}]^{\ell},

\displaystyle(\mathscr{G}_{[2|}^{ij|m+\ell})_{B\{E\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{21}^{{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}})_{B}^{\phantom{B}B^{\prime}}({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{31}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}})_{\{E\}}^{\phantom{\{E\}}\{E^{\prime}\}}{\mathcal{A}}_{123B^{\prime}E^{\prime}}[({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})_{E^{\prime}}]^{\ell-1}.

To rotate to the proper basis starting from (
6.4
) and (
6.5
), one can expand using (
4.3
) as noted above. But it is also possible to expand the special parts of the tensor structures in terms of (
5.10
) with the identities (
B.1
) and then use the contiguous relations (
4.2
) to reach contractions of the type (
B.3
), which can then be easily expressed in terms of objects relevant to the three-point basis (see Appendix
B
).

For (
6.4
), one first rewrites
{\mathcal{A}}_{12B^{\prime}}^{\phantom{12B^{\prime}}F}
in terms of
{\mathcal{A}}_{123B^{\prime}}^{\phantom{123B^{\prime}}F}
and
({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})_{B^{\prime}}({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})^{F}
which leads to

\begin{split}&(\mathscr{G}_{(1|}^{ij|m+\ell})_{\{aA\}\{bB\}\{eE\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\\
&\qquad=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{21}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{j}})_{B}^{\phantom{B}B^{\prime}}({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{31}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}})_{\{E\}}^{\phantom{\{E\}}\{E^{\prime}\}}\left\{(R_{ij,m+\ell}^{-1})_{11}({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})_{B^{\prime}}[({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})_{E^{\prime}}]^{\ell}\right.\\
&\qquad\phantom{=}\left.+(R_{ij,m+\ell}^{-1})_{12}{\mathcal{A}}_{123B^{\prime}E^{\prime}}[({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})_{E^{\prime}}]^{\ell-1}\right\},\end{split}

(6.7)

such that two of the four rotation matrix elements are

\begin{gathered}(R_{ij,m+\ell}^{-1})_{11}=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta,\ell)}^{(d,a_{s}+1/2,0)}(0)-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta-1,\ell)}^{(d,a_{s},0)}(0)+\frac{1}{2}\rho^{(d,1;\Delta/2-a_{s}-\ell/2-1/2)}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta,\ell)}^{(d,a_{s}-1/2,0)}(0),\\
(R_{ij,m+\ell}^{-1})_{12}=\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta,\ell)}^{(d,a_{s},1)}(1).\end{gathered}

(6.8)

Similarly, the first term of (
6.5
) can be rewritten, up to a prefactor, as the projection operators contracted with

{\mathcal{A}}_{13B^{\prime}}^{\phantom{13B^{\prime}}F}{\mathcal{A}}_{13E^{\prime}}^{\phantom{13E^{\prime}}F}{\mathcal{G}}_{(\chi_{m+\ell}+1,\ell-1)\{E^{\prime}\}\{FF\}}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell}-1/2,2)}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3}).

Re-expressing both
{\mathcal{A}}_{13}
in terms of
{\mathcal{A}}_{123}
and
({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})
allows to proceed with the technique of Appendix
B
[the second term of (
6.5
) being trivial], which ultimately yields

\begin{split}&(\mathscr{G}_{(2|}^{ij|m+\ell})_{\{aA\}\{bB\}\{eE\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\\
&\qquad=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{21}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{j}})_{B}^{\phantom{B}B^{\prime}}({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{31}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}})_{\{E\}}^{\phantom{\{E\}}\{E^{\prime}\}}\left\{(R_{ij,m+\ell}^{-1})_{21}({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})_{B^{\prime}}[({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})_{E^{\prime}}]^{\ell}\right.\\
&\qquad\phantom{=}\left.+(R_{ij,m+\ell}^{-1})_{22}{\mathcal{A}}_{123B^{\prime}E^{\prime}}[({\mathcal{A}}_{12}\cdot\tilde{\eta}_{3})_{E^{\prime}}]^{\ell-1}\right\},\end{split}

(6.9)

with the last two rotation matrix elements being given by

\begin{split}&(R_{ij,m+\ell}^{-1})_{21}\\
&\qquad=\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta-1,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s}-1/2,0)}(0)-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s}-1/2,1)}(1)-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s},1)}(1)+\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta+1,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s}+1/2,0)}(0)\\
&\qquad\phantom{=}-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{(d,1;\Delta/2-a_{s}-\ell/2+1/2)}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s}-1,0)}(0)+\frac{1}{4}\rho^{(d,1;\Delta/2-a_{s}-\ell/2+1/2)}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta+1,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s}-1,1)}(1)\\
&\qquad\phantom{=}-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{(d,1;\Delta/2-a_{s}-\ell/2+1/2)}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta+1,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s}-1/2,0)}(0)+\frac{1}{4}\rho^{(d,2;\Delta/2-a_{s}-\ell/2+1/2)}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta+1,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s}-3/2,0)}(0),\\
&(R_{ij,m+\ell}^{-1})_{22}\\
&\qquad=\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s},0)}(0)-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s}-1/2,1)}(1)+\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta+1,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s}-1/2,2)}(0)-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta+1,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s},1)}(1)\\
&\qquad\phantom{=}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta+1,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s}-1/2,2)}(2)+\frac{1}{2}\rho^{(d,1;\Delta/2-a_{s}-\ell/2+1/2)}\tilde{\kappa}_{(\Delta+1,\ell-1)}^{(d,a_{s}-1,1)}(1).\end{split}

(6.10)

Therefore, in the
s
-channel the rotation matrix elements appearing in (
5.16
) are (
6.6
) for
\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{i}{\mathcal{O}}_{j}{\mathcal{O}}_{m}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{SS{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}}\right\rangle
as well as (
6.8
) and (
6.10
) with
i\to l
and
j\to k
(
a_{s}\to b_{s}
) for
\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{l}{\mathcal{O}}_{k}{\mathcal{O}}_{m}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{SV{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}}\right\rangle
.

5.

Repeat the steps above for the
t
-channel
\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{i}{\mathcal{O}}_{l}{\mathcal{O}}_{j}{\mathcal{O}}_{k}}\right\rangle
.

Since
\left\langle{SSSV}\right\rangle
is invariant at the level of the irreducible representations of the external quasi-primary operators under permutation to the
t
-channel, we may directly adapt the above results to the
t
-channel case by making straightforward substitutions. Hence, the rotation matrix elements in the
t
-channel relevant for the bootstrap equations (
5.16
) are (
6.6
) with
j\to l
(
a_{s}\to a_{t}
) for
\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{i}{\mathcal{O}}_{l}{\mathcal{O}}_{m}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{SS{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}}\right\rangle
as well as (
6.8
) and (
6.10
) with
i\to j
and
j\to k
(
a_{s}\to b_{t}
) for
\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{j}{\mathcal{O}}_{k}{\mathcal{O}}_{m}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{SV{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}}\right\rangle
.

6.2
Bootstrap Equations

1.

For both the
s
-channel
\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{i}{\mathcal{O}}_{j}{\mathcal{O}}_{l}{\mathcal{O}}_{k}}\right\rangle
and the
t
-channel
\left\langle{{\mathcal{O}}_{i}{\mathcal{O}}_{l}{\mathcal{O}}_{j}{\mathcal{O}}_{k}}\right\rangle
, compute the four-point conformal blocks using (
3.14
) and the data previously found (projection operators and tensor structures) for each infinite tower of exchanged quasi-primary operators.

Starting with the
s
-channel
\left\langle{SSSV}\right\rangle
, the sole infinite tower of exchanged quasi-primary operators
{\mathcal{O}}^{\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}}
has the projection operators given by (
C.1
) which implies that the expansion (
3.13
) is trivial with only one term (
t=1
and
\ell_{1}=0
) for which
\mathscr{A}_{1}(d,\ell)=1
and
{\hat{{\mathcal{Q}}}}_{23|1}^{{\boldsymbol{0}}}=1
. Therefore, the two different associated four-point conformal blocks are simply

\begin{split}&(\mathscr{G}_{(1|1)}^{ij|m+\ell|lk})_{\{aA\}\{bB\}\{dD\}\{cC\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{4},\eta_{3})\\
&\qquad=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{34}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{k}})_{C}^{\phantom{C}F^{\prime}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)\{\}\{\}\{F^{\prime}\}\{\}}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell},0,0,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{lk,m+\ell},1,0)}}{c_{(d,\ell)}R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell},0)}R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{lk,m+\ell},1)}},\end{split}

(6.11)

and

\begin{split}&(\mathscr{G}_{(1|2)}^{ij|m+\ell|lk})_{\{aA\}\{bB\}\{dD\}\{cC\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{4},\eta_{3})\\
&\qquad=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{34}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{k}})_{C}^{\phantom{C}E^{\prime}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)\{\}\{\}\{\}\{E^{\prime}\}}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell},0,0,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{lk,m+\ell}-1/2,0,1)}}{c_{(d,\ell)}R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{ij,m+\ell},0)}R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{lk,m+\ell}-1/2,0)}},\end{split}

(6.12)

when using the tensor structures (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) in the definition (
3.14
).

Since the irreducible representations of the external quasi-primary operators in the
t
-channel are positioned exactly in the same order, the four-point conformal blocks are analogous with obvious substitutions,
i.e.

\begin{split}&(\mathscr{G}_{(1|1)}^{il|m+\ell|jk})_{\{aA\}\{dD\}\{bB\}\{cC\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{4},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\\
&\qquad=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{32}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{k}})_{C}^{\phantom{C}F^{\prime}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)\{\}\{\}\{F^{\prime}\}\{\}}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{il,m+\ell},0,0,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{jk,m+\ell},1,0)}}{c_{(d,\ell)}R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{il,m+\ell},0)}R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{jk,m+\ell},1)}},\end{split}

(6.13)

and

\begin{split}&(\mathscr{G}_{(1|2)}^{il|m+\ell|jk})_{\{aA\}\{dD\}\{bB\}\{cC\}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{4},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\\
&\qquad=({\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}_{32}^{{\boldsymbol{N}}_{k}})_{C}^{\phantom{C}E^{\prime}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)\{\}\{\}\{\}\{E^{\prime}\}}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{il,m+\ell},0,0,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{jk,m+\ell}-1/2,0,1)}}{c_{(d,\ell)}R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{il,m+\ell},0)}R_{(\chi_{m+\ell},\ell)}^{(d,\chi_{m+\ell}/2+h_{jk,m+\ell}-1/2,0)}}.\end{split}

(6.14)

2.

Perform the
\circ
-product with the help of (
3.16
) to write each four-point conformal block (in both channels) as a linear combination of the four-point tensorial blocks (
4.10
).

It is clear from the four-point conformal blocks in both channels (
6.11
), (
6.12
), (
6.13
) and (
6.14
) that no such
\circ
-product needs to be performed to express all conformal blocks in terms of the four-point tensorial blocks (
4.10
). This occurs since the projection operators for
\ell{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}
are the base projection operators with trivial expansions (
3.13
).

3.

From the free embedding space indices of the external quasi-primary operators in the bootstrap equations (
5.16
), determine the independent set of
{\mathcal{A}}_{12}
,
\epsilon_{12}
,
\Gamma_{12}
,
\bar{\eta}_{3}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}_{12}
and
\bar{\eta}_{4}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}_{12}
contractions which lead to fully scalar four-point bootstrap equations by referring to Table
1
.

For
\left\langle{SSSV}\right\rangle
, we have
{\boldsymbol{0}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{0}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{0}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}={\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}
which implies two independent contractions according to Table
1
. It is straightforward to see that the two contractions of the bootstrap equations (
5.16
) that lead to two independent fully scalar four-point bootstrap equations are given by
(\bar{\eta}_{3}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}_{12})^{C}
and
(\bar{\eta}_{4}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}_{12})^{C}
. It is however more convenient to choose another basis for the contractions, namely
(\bar{\eta}_{1}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}_{34})^{C}
and
(\bar{\eta}_{2}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}_{34})^{C}
, to simplify the computations below.

Put constraints on the OPE coefficients with the help of the bootstrap equations, taking into account conserved current relations between the three-point basis OPE coefficients

Assuming a diagonal metric

\begin{split}&\sum_{m}\sum_{b=1}^{2}{}_{[1|}c_{ijm}{}_{[b|}c_{lkm}v^{\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{j}+\Delta_{k})}(R_{ijm})_{11}\left[(R_{lkm})_{b1}(\ref {Eq4ptCB-s1C1})+(R_{lkm})_{b2}(\ref {Eq4ptCB-s2C1})\right]\\

\begin{split}&\sum_{m}\sum_{b=1}^{2}{}_{[1|}c_{ijm}{}_{[b|}c_{lkm}v^{\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{j}+\Delta_{k})}(R_{ijm})_{11}\left[(R_{lkm})_{b1}(\ref {Eq4ptCB-s1C2})+(R_{lkm})_{b2}(\ref {Eq4ptCB-s2C2})\right]\\

]
.

7
Conclusion

In this work, we developed the conformal bootstrap approach in the context of the embedding space formalism with a modified uplift, originally introduced in
[
62
,
63
]
. Our primary intention was to generate conformal bootstrap equations expressed in terms of standard scalar conformal blocks for spin-
\ell
exchange instead of the scalar conformal blocks for scalar exchange, which is computationally more cumbersome. We accomplished this in Section
3
by expressing the three- and four-point correlation functions obtained from the embedding space OPE in terms of tensorial generalizations of the three- and four-point conformal blocks. Using group theory, we then determined the set of available objects that can be contracted with the (naturally tensorial) bootstrap equations to generate independent scalar conformal bootstrap equations. By deriving a complete set of contiguous relations (Section
4
) in order to reduce the tensorial conformal blocks down to the conformal blocks for symmetric-traceless exchange, we therefore succeeded in expressing the scalar bootstrap equations in terms of standard conformal blocks for spin-
\ell
exchange and functions of the conformal cross-ratios. We also introduced a convenient basis of three-point conformal blocks, or tensor structures, such that the OPE coefficients have trivial symmetry properties under permutations of the three external quasi-primary operators. In particular, in this basis the tensor structures do not mix (Section
5
). Finally, we laid out the complete algorithm leading to independent scalar conformal bootstrap equations directly from the embedding space formalism (
i.e.
without projecting back to position space) in Section
6
. Overall, the algorithm reduces to the following:

1.

Rotation Matrices (Three-Point Correlation Functions)

(a)

Determine the infinite towers of exchanged quasi-primary operators from group theory;

(b)

Find the appropriate projection operators and tensor structures;

(c)

Use (
3.8
) to compute the three-point conformal blocks in terms of the three-point tensorial blocks (
4.1
);

(d)

Re-express the three-point conformal blocks in the proper basis to determine the rotation matrix elements.

2.

Bootstrap Equations (Four-Point Correlation Functions)

(a)

With the information above, compute the four-point conformal blocks (
3.14
);

(b)

Perform some simplifications to express the results in terms of the four-point tensorial blocks (
4.10
);

(c)

From group theory, determine the independent set of contractions leading to scalar bootstrap equations;

(d)

Use the contiguous relations to perform the appropriate contractions obtained previously and obtain independent scalar bootstrap equations in terms of conformal blocks for spin-
\ell
exchange.

Clearly, the method described here requires as input data the projection operators and the tensor structures, which are obtained from group theory.

Compared with our previous technique and other relevant approaches, the main advantages of the present method lie in the final form of the conformal bootstrap equations, which depend only on standard spin-
\ell
conformal blocks, as well as the three-point conformal block basis for which OPE coefficients transform trivially under permutations of the external quasi-primary operators. A key highlight of the method is its built-in capacity to treat all Lorentz representations democratically. This is due to the modified uplift to the embedding space that is at the heart of the approach. It is this salient feature that ultimately allows us to write down the final form of the four-point conformal blocks given in (
3.14
) and (
3.15
). From the form of this result, it is evident that all blocks corresponding to arbitrary exchanged representations and external quasi-primary operators have the same form, up to a choice of input data, in particular the projection operators and tensor structures, as well as a handful of parameters that may be fixed from the input data, such as
\{n_{v}^{m},i_{a},i_{b},\ell_{t}\}
. One possible shortcoming is the form of the rotation matrices. Although the computation of the rotation matrix is constructive and as such fairly straightforward, it does however leave us with potentially complicated rotation matrix elements. This is reminiscent of other approaches, such as the weight-shifting method
[
44
]
, where potentially cumbersome coefficients arise for a similar reason, namely due to a change of the three-point basis. Further, the explicit appearance of differential operators inside the contiguous relations make them more complicated in form. Nevertheless, they do lead to bootstrap equations expressed in terms of standard spin-
\ell
-exchange blocks, which is not always straightforward to obtain from related approaches (
e.g.
the Casimir equation method
[
45
]
). Although the first-order differential operators that potentially appear in the final form of the conformal blocks may in principle be removed via some identities due to Dolan and Osborn (Appendix
A
), it is an open question whether the resulting linear combination of conformal blocks is easier to handle computationally as opposed to the direct action of the differential operators. We hope to address some of these shortcomings and concerns in future work.

It is natural to attempt to harness the method presented here for the study of correlation functions of conserved currents or energy-momentum tensors. Indeed, the energy-momentum tensor is the only non-trivial local quasi-primary operator that exists in all sufficiently local CFTs. It is therefore expected that the conformal bootstrap of four energy-momentum tensors could lead to interesting results on the central charge.
